```text
144	\label{app:robustness_checks}
145	
146	Table~\ref{tab:robustness_checks} summarizes regression results across model subsets and alternative specifications at $K{=}5$, testing whether the predictor shift is robust to collinearity reduction and specification choice.
147	
148	\begin{table}[h]
149	\centering
150	\small
151	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
152	\toprule
153	\textbf{Subset / Method} & $\boldsymbol{r}$ & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Trans.} \\
154	\midrule
155	Full bootstrap       & 0.81 & 2/12 & 7/12 & \checkmark \\
156	API-only (9) clust.  & 0.85 & 8/12 & 3/12 & --- \\
157	Cap-matched A (5A+4L) & 0.50 & ---  & 9/12 & \checkmark\checkmark \\
158	Cap-matched B (6A+3L) & 0.73 & ---  & 2/12 & --- \\
159	Fractional logit     & 0.81 & 4/12 & 11/12 & \checkmark \\
160	\bottomrule
161	\end{tabular}
162	\caption{Predictor shift robustness at $K{=}5$ across model subsets and specifications. $r$: correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$. $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ columns show FDR-significant counts out of 12 conditions. Trans.: whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates $\eta_{\max}$. API-only models ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) show partially collinear predictors; capability-matched subsamples ($r{=}0.50$) strengthen the shift. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} confirms OLS patterns with 98.6\% directional agreement (71/72 direction matches; the single discordance is $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}7$ for ARC $\times$ score manipulation).}
163	\label{tab:robustness_checks}
164	\end{table}
165	
166	The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the predictor shift does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, comparable to the full-pool 7/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the shift weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
167	
168	\section{Random Subsample Analysis}
169	\label{app:random_subsample}
170	
171	To test whether the predictor shift depends on model pool composition, we randomly sample 9 of 15 models (1{,}000 trials), construct all $\binom{9}{5}$ panels, and run the bootstrap analysis at $K{=}5$. We stratify by the number of local models ($n_{\mathrm{local}} \in \{0, \ldots, 6\}$) in each subsample.
172	
173	Under clustered standard errors, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is the dominant predictor in 51.4\% of subsamples (mean significance 1.85/12), with an inverted-U as a function of $n_{\mathrm{local}}$: subsamples with 3--4 local models (maximizing capability range) achieve the highest $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance rate, while all-API ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{=}0$) and heavily-local ($n_{\mathrm{local}}{\geq}5$) subsamples show reduced detection. Significance is attenuated relative to the full pool by smaller sample sizes (126 vs.\ 3{,}003 panels at $K{=}5$), but the shift direction holds in the majority of subsamples. This confirms that the shift's detectability depends on capability diversity in the model pool rather than pool size per se.
```

```text
445	\section{Gaussian Copula Assumption and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Robustness}
446	\label{app:copula_gof}
447	
448	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ admits a closed-form expression $K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$ under the Gaussian copula, where $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j)) = 1 - \rho_{ij}^2$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}. We test whether this parametric assumption holds for judge pairs using the \citet{genest2008validity} Cram\'{e}r--von Mises goodness-of-fit test for Gaussian copulas.
449	
450	\paragraph{GoF Results.} Across all $\binom{15}{2} = 105$ judge pairs, the Gaussian copula is rejected at $\alpha{=}0.05$ for 97--100\% of pairs (MMLU: 102/105, 97.1\%; ARC: 104/105, 99.0\%). The rejections are not marginal: median $p$-values are ${<}0.001$ on both benchmarks. This indicates that the pairwise dependence structure among LLM judges is substantially non-Gaussian, likely reflecting the discrete three-category nature of winner labels and the heavy tail structure of judge agreement patterns.
451	
452	\paragraph{Ranking Robustness Despite Copula Rejection.} Despite the Gaussian copula being formally rejected, the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $1 - \rho_{ij}^2$) and the plug-in MI $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (using $\exp(-2\,\widehat{\mathrm{MI}}(i,j))$ with Miller--Madow correction) produce highly concordant panel rankings within each $K$. Within-$K$ Spearman rank correlations are $\rho \approx 0.86$ ($K{=}3$: 0.862, $K{=}5$: 0.858, $K{=}7$: 0.867). The systematic level difference (plug-in $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ mean 3.38 vs.\ closed-form 4.29, averaged across $K$) reflects the Miller--Madow bias correction increasing MI estimates, which compresses $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ toward zero. This level shift does not affect regression results, which depend on ranking.
453	
454	\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.
455	
```

</details>

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Implications.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effectiveness as a predictor of panel vulnerability does not depend on the Gaussian copula being correct. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI})$ kernel provides a nonparametric, information-theoretic measure of pairwise alienation; the Gaussian copula merely offers a convenient closed-form approximation that preserves ranking fidelity. All regression analyses in the main text use the closed-form $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; the plug-in MI version would yield equivalent conclusions for any analysis based on panel ordering.\n\n\\section{Quantization Dummy Regression}\n\\label{app:quant_dummy}\n\nTo disentangle the AWQ quantization confound from the diversity signal, we augment Equation~\\ref{eq:regression} with a binary dummy $D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$ indicating whether the panel's weakest judge is a quantized local model. The augmented regression is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon) + \\beta_3 D_{\\mathrm{quant}}.\n\\end{equation}\nAdding the dummy attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s standardized coefficient from $+0.39$ ($K{=}3$) to $+0.34$ ($K{=}5$) to $+0.27$ ($K{=}7$), with FDR-significant counts of 6/12, 6/12, and 4/12 respectively. The attenuation confirms partial confounding: some of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s explanatory power reflects the capability gap between quantized and API models rather than independence per se. However, collinearity between $D_{\\mathrm{quant}}$ and both predictors escalates sharply with panel size (VIF${=}162$ at $K{=}7$), rendering the dummy unreliable for large panels. The quantization dummy analysis is therefore informative at $K{=}3$ (moderate VIF) but should be interpreted cautiously at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\section{Bayesian Posterior Concentration Analysis}\n\\label{app:bayesian_entropy}\n\nBayesian calibration neutralizes $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}7$ (0/12 significant; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). One hypothesized mechanism is that the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing effective panel diversity. We test this by computing the posterior concentration ratio $C = \\max_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}} / \\min_j w_j^{\\mathrm{post}}$ across panels.\n\nThe concentration ratio declines modestly across panel sizes: $C = 0.890$ ($K{=}3$), $0.874$ ($K{=}5$), $0.867$ ($K{=}7$), a total change of only 2.3 percentage points. This gradual decline is insufficient to explain the abrupt neutralization at $K{=}7$ (from 6/12 at $K{=}3$ to 0/12 at $K{=}7$). The mechanism is therefore not simple posterior concentration; rather, the interaction between Bayesian weighting and the combinatorial structure of larger panels likely drives the neutralization pattern. Full characterization of this interaction remains an open question."
}
```

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

[Director 指令] [Patch] **R5 剩余修复项 — Conclusion 扩展 + Related Work 补引用 + OpenRouter footnote**

三个独立修改：

1. **Conclusion 扩展 (R5-W1)**：当前 Conclusion 只有 3 行，需扩展到 1/3~1/2 column。增加内容：
   - Practical implications：小 panel (K=3) 优先筛选 η_max，大 panel (K≥5) 需关注 judge independence
   - 方法论 takeaway：predictor shift 是 method-dependent 的（bootstrap 支持，fractional logit 不支持 — 9/9/9 flat），diversity-capability confound 本身是一个发现
   - Future work：capability-controlled designs（训练时控制 diversity）、open-ended benchmarks、causal identification
   - 保持描述性语气，不做因果声明

2. **Related Work 补引用 (R5-W3)**：加入以下 4 篇，每篇 1 句话融入现有段落：
   - Li et al. 2024 (LLM judge diversity) — 加入 LLM-as-Judge 段落
   - Zhu et al. 2020 DVERGE (training-time diversity for adversarial robustness) — 加入 Adversarial Robustness 段落，注明 training-time diversity vs 我们的 post-hoc selection 区别
   - Cameron & Miller 2015 (cluster-robust inference guide) — 加入 methodology 或作为 Limitations 的引用
   - MacKinnon & Webb 2017 (wild bootstrap with few clusters) — 同上

3. **OpenRouter footnote (R5-M1)**：在 Methodology section 中 API model 首次提及处加 footnote，报告数据采集时间窗口："All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between [Month] 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period."（具体月份写 "March--April 2026"）

约束：
- 主文正文 ≤ 8 页（Limitations 不计入）
- Abstract ≤ 200 words（已修改，不要再改 abstract）
- 引用需要验证真实性
- 不改 abstract 和 introduction

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}
4	LLM judges gained traction after \citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under adversarial attacks, and \citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \citet{dawid1979maximum}; \citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. \citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\% to 10--19\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent systems but focus on agent coordination; \citet{qian2026collabeval} propose collaborative multi-phase evaluation but assume benign conditions. We test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.
5	
6	\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}
7	\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\geq}5$.
8	
9	\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}
10	\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}
13	Adversarial vulnerability was formalized by \citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.
14	
15	\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}
16	Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.
17	
18	\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}
19	Information-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.
20	
```

```text
1	\section{Experimental Methodology}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}
5	
6	We select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\,GB). API models are assigned to \emph{frontier}, \emph{mid-tier}, or \emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \lceil K/2 \rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\times$ item $\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \S\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.
7	
8	\begin{table}[t]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{lllccc}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Tier} & \textbf{Deploy} & \textbf{MMLU} & \textbf{ARC} \\
15	\midrule
16	gpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\
17	gemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\
18	claude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\
19	gemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\dagger$ \\
20	\addlinespace
21	gpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\
22	claude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\
23	\addlinespace
24	gpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\
25	gpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\
26	claude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\
27	mistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\
28	llama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\
29	llama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\
30	qwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\
31	qwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\
32	qwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\
33	\bottomrule
34	\end{tabular}}
35	\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\dagger$\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\%, lightweight ${\leq}$80\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).}
36	\label{tab:models}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}
40	
41	Two benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\ref{app:temperature}).
42	
43	Each benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\ref{app:attack_details}).
44	
45	\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}
46	
47	Per-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\eta_j(a) = \Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\eta_{\max}(a) = \max_{j \in \mathcal{P}} \eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\exp(-2\,\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\mathrm{eff}} = K - \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i}\sum_{j \neq i} \rho_{ij}^2$ \citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\rho{\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.
48	
49	\subsection{Aggregation Methods}
50	
51	Beyond majority vote ($\geq \lceil K/2 \rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \propto \mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\sum_j w_j \cdot \mathbf{1}[J_j \text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\text{correct} \mid \mathbf{v}) \propto P(\text{correct}) \prod_j P(v_j \mid \text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).
52	
53	\subsection{Statistical Framework}
54	\label{sec:stat_framework}
55	
56	To disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:
57	\begin{equation}
58	\label{eq:regression}
59	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
60	\end{equation}
61	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.
62	
63	Dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).
64	
65	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
66	\label{sec:robustness_method}
67	
68	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\leq}11$, ${\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}).
69

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MSvsa2npiqTywMjKAUmypk`</summary>

```text
38	\input{conclusion}
39	
40	\section*{Limitations}
41	
42	Our study has several limitations. \textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.
43	
44	\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\leq}9$ position clusters) \citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.
45	
46	\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.
47	
48	\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.
49	
50	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
51	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
52	
```

The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n",

      "startLine": 38,
      "totalLines": 59
    }

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe document a predictor shift: at $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (7/12, 8/12) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ attenuates (2/12), with positive direction (12/12) but method-sensitive significance (0--12/12; fractional logit 9/12 across all $K$). The confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; RV insufficient in 69\\%) limits causal claims: screen $\\eta_{\\max}$ at $K{=}3$, monitor independence at $K{\\geq}5$.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe document a predictor shift in LLM judge panel vulnerability: at $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute to predicting panel attack success; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates (bootstrap 7/12, 8/12) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ attenuates (2/12, 1/12). The positive association between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR holds across all 12 conditions (12/12 direction), but significance is method-dependent: wild cluster bootstrap supports the shift (4/12 $\\to$ 7/12), while fractional logit yields a flat 9/12 across all $K$. The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; Cinelli--Hazlett RV insufficient in 69\\% of conditions) is itself a substantive finding: in current LLM ecosystems, diverse panels are structurally more capable, making observational separation of the two channels unreliable.\n\nFor practitioners, these results suggest a two-regime strategy: at small panel sizes ($K{=}3$), screening for $\\eta_{\\max}$ remains the primary defense, as a single compromised judge flips the majority; at $K{\\geq}5$, monitoring inter-judge independence becomes important, though the confound means that low-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels may simply contain weaker judges. Bayesian calibration offers partial mitigation but does not fully neutralize the diversity channel.\n\nFuture work should pursue capability-controlled designs---training or fine-tuning judges to decouple diversity from capability---to enable causal identification. Extending the framework to open-ended generation benchmarks and larger model pools would test whether the predictor shift generalizes beyond multiple-choice evaluation."
}
```

    "newString": "Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving}, diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving}, or vulnerability diversification \\citep{zhu2020dverge} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision but requires coordinating member training---a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under adversarial attacks, and \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent systems but focus on agent coordination; \\citet{qian2026collabeval} propose collaborative multi-phase evaluation but assume benign conditions. We test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. We focus on the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure} of this vulnerability: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Our setting differs: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges. We address adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API; six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}).\n",

_source line 2252 · 2026-05-24T19:34:10.895Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] **β_std 效应大小轨迹整合 (R1-W1-v3 关键修复)**

这是 R5 最重要的未完成修复：将 predictor shift 的主要证据从 significance count 转为 effect size (β_std) 轨迹。

在 results.tex §4.2 "Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift" 中，Figure 5 描述之后，添加一段 β_std 轨迹说明：

数据：
- η_max β_std 跨 K 下降：0.266 (K=3) → 0.101 (K=5) → 0.084 (K=7)
- K_eff β_std 跨 K 稳定/略升：~0.35 (K=3) → ~0.44 (K=5) → ~0.39 (K=7)
- 这种 effect size 分离比 significance count 更稳健（不受 power/sample size 影响）

叙事要点：
- "Effect sizes corroborate the significance-based shift: η_max's standardized coefficient declines monotonically (0.266 → 0.101 → 0.084) while K_eff's remains stable (0.35 → 0.44 → 0.39), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift."
- 这解决了 Reviewer 对 low cluster count / power artifact 的质疑 — effect size 不受样本量影响

约束：不要增加超过 3 行正文。主文 ≤ 8 页。

87	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ for $\eta_{\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \to 0.437 \to 0.401$), consistent with a shift in explanatory weight rather than mere significance-count patterns. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.
88	
89	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
90	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
91	
92	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).

Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ for $\\eta_{\\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \\to 0.437 \\to 0.401$), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift that does not depend on sample size or cluster count. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.",
          " ",
          " \\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}",
          " \\label{sec:rf_contrast}"
        ]
      }

{
  "type": "queue-operation",
  "operation": "enqueue",
  "timestamp": "2026-05-25T08:05:02.053Z",
  "sessionId": "fd5a1623-4ec8-475e-b94c-cd2b56a8426c",
  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch — R6 审稿修复 + 叙事重构]\n\n投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 via ARR。DDL 紧迫，集中写作修复。引用需要验证。\n\n## 背景\n\nR6 审稿 Overall 3.0 (Findings)，上界 3.5。经过 κ-diversity 替代回归 + 合成数据验证 + meta-analytic pooling 三组分析，论文核心叙事需要重构。\n\n## 叙事重构方向\n\n**从\"Shifting Predictor\"转向\"Diminishing Weakest Link\"：**\n\n核心故事变更：随着 panel 增大，individual judge weakness (η_max) 的预测力单调递减（β_std 0.266→0.101→0.084，meta-analytic CI 在 K=7 跨零），而 diversity-related vulnerability 持续存在（方向一致正，pooled CI 排除零）。大 panel 安全瓶颈从\"最弱的 judge\"转向\"panel composition\"。\n\n**Robust findings（可作核心贡献）：**\n1. Diversity 方向一致正（K_eff 和 κ-diversity 都确认 diversity 增加 vulnerability）\n2. η_max 效应随 K 单调递减（两个 diversity 指标规格下都成立，meta-analytic 确认）\n3. Aggregation modulates predictability（Claim 7，不受影响）\n4. Monte Carlo WCB 保守性（null rejection ≤1.6%，sig counts 是下界）\n\n**Non-robust（降级为 observation）：**\n- Diversity significance 的 K-dependent trajectory 取决于 diversity 指标选择（K_eff 3→7→8 递增，κ-diversity 8→7→6 递减）。报告为 metric sensitivity finding，不作 PRIMARY contribution。\n\n## 具体修改清单（按优先级）\n\n### P1. 标题更换\n当前：\"The Shifting Predictor: Panel-Size-Dependent Vulnerability Patterns in LLM Judge Panels\"\n建议：\"The Diminishing Weakest Link: Panel Size and the Shifting Drivers of Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels\"\n或更简洁的变体。\n\n### P2. Abstract 重写\n- 核心 claim 从 \"predictor shift（K_eff dominance 递增）\" 改为 \"η_max 预测力随 K 递减 + diversity 方向一致正\"\n- 加 Monte Carlo 保守性一句\n- 保持 ≤200 words\n- 删除/替换因果语言\n\n### P3. Intro Contribution 重构\n- Contribution 1：diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability（两个 diversity 指标确认）\n- Contribution 2：η_max 单调递减（meta-analytic pooled CI 在 K=7 跨零）\n- Contribution 3：aggregation gradient（不变）\n- 删除 predictor shift 作为独立 contribution\n- Theorem 1 降级为 Proposition 1 / Supporting Lemma\n\n### P4. 系统性因果语言清理（复发 ×4，必须根治）\n全文搜索并替换：\n- determines → predicts / is associated with\n- mechanism → pattern / statistical association\n- pathway → predictor / correlate\n- drives → is associated with\n- causes/shapes/governs → correlates with\n- leads to / induces → is associated with\n- effect of / impact of → association between / relationship between\n- role of X in driving → association of X with\n- responsible for → associated with\n- contributes to → is correlated with\n- explains why → is consistent with\n扩大搜索范围包括 Figure captions（尤其 Figure 1 \"determines vulnerability\" → \"predicts vulnerability\"）\n\n### P5. Section 5 / Results 叙事转换\n- 主线从 sig count (X/12) 转为 meta-analytic pooled β_std + CI\n- 报告格式：\"pooled K_eff β_std = 0.356 [0.267, 0.445] at K=3, significantly positive (DL random-effects, I²=87.7%)\"\n- η_max 衰减用 pooled CI 量化：\"η_max pooled β_std declines from 0.264 [0.158, 0.369] at K=3 to 0.084 [-0.083, 0.251] at K=7, becoming non-significant\"\n- I² 解读：K_eff 高 I² 但方向一致 → 攻击间效应大小不同但方向一致；η_max K=7 I²=99.8% + 5/12 负系数 → 方向不稳定\n\n### P6. κ-diversity 对比 + Metric Sensitivity 讨论\n- 在 §5 或 §6 新增 1 段（~150 words）：报告 κ-diversity 替代回归的 shift 方向相反（8→7→6 vs 3→7→8），讨论 metric sensitivity\n- 引导结论：diversity 效应方向 robust，但 significance trajectory 是 operationalization-dependent\n- 合成数据验证放 Appendix（WCB 方法论 soundness 证据）\n\n### P7. Monte Carlo 保守性写入主文\n在 §4 methodology 或 §4.1 开头加一句：\n\"Monte Carlo calibration (1000 permutations, B=999) confirms WCB does not over-reject in any of 36 conditions (max null rejection rate = 1.6% at nominal α=5%), establishing reported significance counts as conservative lower bounds.\"\n\n### P8. _source line 2281 · 2026-05-25T08:05:02.234Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch — R6 审稿修复 + 叙事重构]

投稿目标：EMNLP 2026 via ARR。DDL 紧迫，集中写作修复。引用需要验证。

## 背景

R6 审稿 Overall 3.0 (Findings)，上界 3.5。经过 κ-diversity 替代回归 + 合成数据验证 + meta-analytic pooling 三组分析，论文核心叙事需要重构。

## 叙事重构方向

**从"Shifting Predictor"转向"Diminishing Weakest Link"：**

核心故事变更：随着 panel 增大，individual judge weakness (η_max) 的预测力单调递减（β_std 0.266→0.101→0.084，meta-analytic CI 在 K=7 跨零），而 diversity-related vulnerability 持续存在（方向一致正，pooled CI 排除零）。大 panel 安全瓶颈从"最弱的 judge"转向"panel composition"。

**Robust findings（可作核心贡献）：**
1. Diversity 方向一致正（K_eff 和 κ-diversity 都确认 diversity 增加 vulnerability）
2. η_max 效应随 K 单调递减（两个 diversity 指标规格下都成立，meta-analytic 确认）
3. Aggregation modulates predictability（Claim 7，不受影响）
4. Monte Carlo WCB 保守性（null rejection ≤1.6%，sig counts 是下界）

**Non-robust（降级为 observation）：**
- Diversity significance 的 K-dependent trajectory 取决于 diversity 指标选择（K_eff 3→7→8 递增，κ-diversity 8→7→6 递减）。报告为 metric sensitivity finding，不作 PRIMARY contribution。

## 具体修改清单（按优先级）

### P1. 标题更换
当前："The Shifting Predictor: Panel-Size-Dependent Vulnerability Patterns in LLM Judge Panels"
建议："The Diminishing Weakest Link: Panel Size and the Shifting Drivers of Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels"
或更简洁的变体。

### P2. Abstract 重写
- 核心 claim 从 "predictor shift（K_eff dominance 递增）" 改为 "η_max 预测力随 K 递减 + diversity 方向一致正"
- 加 Monte Carlo 保守性一句
- 保持 ≤200 words
- 删除/替换因果语言

### P3. Intro Contribution 重构
- Contribution 1：diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability（两个 diversity 指标确认）
- Contribution 2：η_max 单调递减（meta-analytic pooled CI 在 K=7 跨零）
- Contribution 3：aggregation gradient（不变）
- 删除 predictor shift 作为独立 contribution
- Theorem 1 降级为 Proposition 1 / Supporting Lemma

### P4. 系统性因果语言清理（复发 ×4，必须根治）
全文搜索并替换：
- determines → predicts / is associated with
- mechanism → pattern / statistical association
- pathway → predictor / correlate
- drives → is associated with
- causes/shapes/governs → correlates with
- leads to / induces → is associated with
- effect of / impact of → association between / relationship between
- role of X in driving → association of X with
- responsible for → associated with
- contributes to → is correlated with
- explains why → is consistent with
扩大搜索范围包括 Figure captions（尤其 Figure 1 "determines vulnerability" → "predicts vulnerability"）

### P5. Section 5 / Results 叙事转换
- 主线从 sig count (X/12) 转为 meta-analytic pooled β_std + CI
- 报告格式："pooled K_eff β_std = 0.356 [0.267, 0.445] at K=3, significantly positive (DL random-effects, I²=87.7%)"
- η_max 衰减用 pooled CI 量化："η_max pooled β_std declines from 0.264 [0.158, 0.369] at K=3 to 0.084 [-0.083, 0.251] at K=7, becoming non-significant"
- I² 解读：K_eff 高 I² 但方向一致 → 攻击间效应大小不同但方向一致；η_max K=7 I²=99.8% + 5/12 负系数 → 方向不稳定

### P6. κ-diversity 对比 + Metric Sensitivity 讨论
- 在 §5 或 §6 新增 1 段（~150 words）：报告 κ-diversity 替代回归的 shift 方向相反（8→7→6 vs 3→7→8），讨论 metric sensitivity
- 引导结论：diversity 效应方向 robust，但 significance trajectory 是 operationalization-dependent
- 合成数据验证放 Appendix（WCB 方法论 soundness 证据）

### P7. Monte Carlo 保守性写入主文
在 §4 methodology 或 §4.1 开头加一句：
"Monte Carlo calibration (1000 permutations, B=999) confirms WCB does not over-reject in any of 36 conditions (max null rejection rate = 1.6% at nominal α=5%), establishing reported significance counts as conservative lower bounds."

### P8. Related Work 扩展
补充经典 ensemble diversity 文献并实质讨论：
- Breiman 2001 (Random Forests)
- Dietterich 2000 (Ensemble Methods)
- Zhou, Wu & Tang 2002 (Ensembling Neural Networks)
- Blanchard et al. 2017 (Byzantine Tolerant Gradient Descent)
- JudgeBench (Tan et al. 2024), Auto-J (Li et al. 2024)
明确论证 LLM 评审面板 vs 经典 ensemble 的独特性（adversarial manipulation vs random noise，heterogeneous models vs homogeneous weak classifiers）

### P9. K_eff 正系数 vs BFT/Condorcet 讨论
Section 5.1/5.3 扩展 1-2 段：random faults 下独立性是保护（Condorcet），adversarial 下独立性是攻击面。引用 Blanchard et al. 2017。

### P10. Bayesian R²=0.09 讨论
充分讨论 R²=0.09 含义：composition 在 Bayesian 下几乎无预测力 → predictor 可能仅在特定聚合条件下有效。Random vs adversarial R² 对比加 bootstrap CI（数据已有）。

### P11. 数字修正
- Fractional logit：全文确认为 9/12 at ALL K（不是 11/12 或 12/12）
- Adaptive R²：0.275 → 0.256
- CRVE 数字：确认论文中 CRVE 报告与实际数据一致（K3: 0/12, K5: 8/12, K7: 8/12）

### P12. Conclusion 前瞻
加 2-3 句前瞻性讨论（模型能力提升后 shift 趋势、MC → open-ended 扩展）。Theorem 1 降级为 Proposition 后可腾出空间。

### P13. Scope Qualifier 强化
Title/Abstract/Claims 更明确限定 "multiple-choice evaluation"。Limitations 增加一句讨论 MC 结构特殊性（discrete options, fixed answer space vs open-ended continuous scoring）。

### P14. Minor 修复
- Figure 编号不连续（缺 Figure 7）→ 修复
- Figure 1 文字标签字号调大
- Figure 9 子图字号 <6pt → 调大
- 页眉 "Anonymous ACL submission" 确认是否需要调整

## 页面预算约束
正文严格 ≤8 页。当前刚好卡在 8 页边界。任何增加（κ-diversity 讨论、Condorcet 讨论、Conclusion 扩展）都需要从其他地方压缩。Theorem 1 → Proposition 1 + 删除证明可腾空间。合成数据 + Monte Carlo 详细数据放 Appendix。

## 实验完成度
核心分析全部完成。FP16 baseline 仍在 GPU 上跑，不阻塞写作——如未完成在 Limitations 中说明。

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}
5	\label{sec:dominance}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\ref{tab:models}).
8	
9	Under OLS, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\eta_{\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\ref{app:keff_dist}).
10	
11	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
12	
13	\begin{table}[t]
14	\centering
15	\small
16	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
17	\toprule
18	\textbf{Method} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ \textbf{sig} & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ \textbf{sig} \\
19	\midrule
20	OLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\
21	Clustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\
22	Mixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\
23	Wild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\
24	Frac.\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\
25	\bottomrule
26	\end{tabular}
27	\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}
28	\label{tab:method_comparison}
29	\end{table}
30	
31	\begin{figure}[t]
32	\centering
33	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
34	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
35	\label{fig:robustness}
36	\end{figure}
37	
38	Power analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
39	
40	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}
41	\label{sec:cross_k}
42	
43	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).
44	
45	We repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
46	
47	\begin{figure}[t]
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
50	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
51	\label{fig:regime_transition}
52	\end{figure}
53	
54	\begin{figure}[t]
55	\centering
56	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}
57	\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\eta_{\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\eta_{\max}$ disappears.}
58	\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}
59	\end{figure}
60	
61	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ for $\eta_{\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \to 0.437 \to 0.401$), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift that does not depend on sample size or cluster count. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.
62	
63	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
64	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
65	
66	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).
67	
68	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
69	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
70	
71	GAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\% positive rate; Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).
72	
73	\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}
74	\label{sec:aggregation}
75	
76	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
77	
78	\begin{table}[t]
79	\centering
80	\small
81	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
82	\toprule
83	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\
84	\midrule
85	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\
86	Majority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\
87	Weighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\
88	Bayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\
89	\bottomrule
90	\end{tabular}
91	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}
92	\label{tab:aggregation}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	
96	Predictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}).
97	
98	\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}
99	Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
100

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	\usepackage{times}
4	\usepackage{latexsym}
5	\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
6	\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
7	\usepackage{microtype}

11	\usepackage{multirow}
