16	
17	\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}
18	\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}
19	\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}
20	
21	\title{The Shifting Predictor: Panel-Size-Dependent Vulnerability\\Patterns in LLM Judge Panels}
22	
23	\author{Anonymous}
24	
25	\begin{document}
26	\maketitle
27	
28	\begin{abstract}
29	Multi-judge panels are widely deployed for robust Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation, yet adding judges can shift, not reduce, the dominant vulnerability pathway. Existing defenses conflate panel diversity with individual judge robustness, and it remains unclear which factor drives attack success rate (ASR) reduction. We decompose panel ASR into weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under ordinary least squares, but significance varies across inference methods (0 to 12 of 12), and the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal claims. The dominant predictor shifts with panel size: at $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates while $\eta_{\max}$ attenuates. Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Small panels benefit most from individual robustness screening; larger panels require attention to judge independence.
30	\end{abstract}
31	
32	\input{introduction}
33	\input{related_work}
34	\input{threat_model}
35	\input{methodology}
36	\input{results}
37	\input{analysis}
38	\input{conclusion}
39	
40	\section*{Limitations}
41	
42	Our study has several limitations. \textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.
43	
44	\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\leq}9$ position clusters) \citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.
45	
46	\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.
47	
48	\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.
49	
50	\section*{Code and Data Availability}
51	Code, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.
52	
53	\input{appendix}
54	\input{appendix_attacks}
55

Existing defenses conflate panel diversity with individual judge robustness, and it remains unclear which factor drives attack success rate (ASR) reduction. We decompose panel ASR into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under ordinary least squares, but significance varies across inference methods (0 to 12 of 12), and the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal claims. The dominant predictor shifts with panel size: at $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates while $\\eta_{\\max}$ attenuates. Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Small panels benefit most from individual robustness screening; larger panels require attention to judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations. \\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n",
      "numLines": 55,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 59
    }

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.93\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\eta_{\max}$ dominates. \textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \in (3,5)$. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
11	
12	The conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.
13	
14	We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\times$ six attacks), $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}).
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 across all $K$) (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
21	\item \textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
22	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
25	Our findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.
26	
```

    "oldString": "\\title{The Shifting Predictor: Panel-Size-Dependent Vulnerability\\\\Patterns in LLM Judge Panels}",
    "newString": "\\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: How Panel Size Shifts\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability Drivers in LLM Judge Panels}",
    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\\usepackage{tikz}\n\\usetikzlibrary{shapes.geometric,arrows.meta,positioning,fit,shadows.blur,calc}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Shifting Predictor: Panel-Size-Dependent Vulnerability\\\\Patterns in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely deployed for robust Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation, yet adding judges can shift, not reduce, the dominant vulnerability pathway. Existing defenses conflate panel diversity with individual judge robustness, and it remains unclear which factor drives attack success rate (ASR) reduction. We decompose panel ASR into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under ordinary least squares, but significance varies across inference methods (0 to 12 of 12), and the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal claims. The dominant predictor shifts with panel size: at $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates while $\\eta_{\\max}$ attenuates. Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Small panels benefit most from individual robustness screening; larger panels require attention to judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations. \\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability in current LLM ecosystems ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) is a fundamental limitation: observational regression cannot reliably separate their contributions. Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

63	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections \citep[see][for a practitioner's guide]{cameron2015practitioners}: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\leq}11$, ${\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}).
64

    "newString": "Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}). Monte Carlo calibration (1{,}000 permutations, $B{=}999$) confirms the wild cluster bootstrap does not over-reject in any of 36 conditions (max null rejection rate ${=}1.6\\%$ at nominal $\\alpha{=}5\\%$), establishing reported significance counts as conservative lower bounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX~6000 Ada (48\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types: (1)~\\textbf{prompt injection}, adversarial instructions overriding the evaluation prompt \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}; (2)~\\textbf{sycophancy}, flattering language exploiting judge preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; (3)~\\textbf{score manipulation}, explicit numerical cues anchoring scoring; (4)~\\textbf{verbosity bias}, padding with irrelevant detail \\citep{zheng2023judging}; (5)~\\textbf{position bias}, reordering to exploit positional preferences \\citep{wang2023fair}; and (6)~\\textbf{authority bias}, fabricated credentials inflating credibility. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attack_details}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability under attack $a$ is the fraction of items where judge $j$ is fooled: $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$, estimated empirically over the item set. The weakest-link vulnerability $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the most susceptible member of each panel. The panel attack success rate $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ is the fraction of items where at least $t$ judges are simultaneously fooled. Panel diversity is quantified by $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}), computed from pairwise MI between judges' three-category winner labels (model~A wins, model~B wins, tie) on clean data, using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow bias correction. The $\\exp(-2\\,\\mathrm{MI}(i,j))$ term measures pairwise alienation: 1 when independent, 0 under full dependence. Under a Gaussian copula this yields $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the Gaussian copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet plug-in MI and closed-form rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). Together, $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link channel) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (diversity channel) serve as the two predictors in the regression framework below.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's average incremental contribution across all subsets. Generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies between targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity, authority bias).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections \\citep[see][for a practitioner's guide]{cameron2015practitioners}: clustered standard errors (SE; CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}).\n",

Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ for $\\eta_{\\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \\to 0.437 \\to 0.401$), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift that does not depend on sample size or cluster count. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

{

  "parentUuid": "8f597666-a19f-429d-97a7-13438f5f66d1",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "7d23653d-28be-43ed-85f3-54288a8d6755",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "R^2 = 0.275",
    "newString": "R^2 = 0.256",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 across all $K$) (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.\n",

      {

        "oldLines": 7,

Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).",

Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.275$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).",

18	This departs from BFT models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.
19	
20	\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}]
21	\label{thm:monotonicity}
22	Replacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.
23	\end{theorem}
24	
25	The theorem predicts $\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\ref{eq:regression}. The independence-based intuition suggests $\beta_1 \leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\eta_{\max}$ versus $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \in \{3,5,7\}$ (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
26

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HAkkQrbYJnSTdrXHjErD73`</summary>

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}}
4	\label{app:proof}
5	
6	The replaced judge $J_K'$ is \emph{pivotal} exactly when $t{-}1$ of the remaining $K{-}1$ judges have already failed. Formally, let $S = \sum_{j=1}^{K-1} X_j^a$ denote the number of failures among the shared judges. The panel fails if and only if the $K$-th judge also fails when $S = t{-}1$, or when $S \geq t$ regardless of the $K$-th judge:
7	\begin{align}
8	\mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a) &= \Pr(S \geq t) + \Pr(S = t{-}1) \cdot \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid S = t{-}1).
9	\end{align}
10	Since $\Pr(X_{K'}^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K}) \geq \Pr(X_K^a {=} 1 \mid \mathbf{X}_{-K}^a {=} \mathbf{x}_{-K})$ for all attacks $a$ and realizations $\mathbf{x}_{-K}$ (by hypothesis), the second term is weakly larger for $\mathcal{P}'$ than for $\mathcal{P}$ under every attack. Taking the supremum over attacks preserves the inequality: $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}') \geq \mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P})$. The result holds under arbitrary inter-judge correlations and any attack space $\mathcal{A}$.
```

The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts",

The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nThe proposition predicts",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information (MI) among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from BFT models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \\emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Weakest-Link Monotonicity; formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}]\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones. The stochastic dominance condition holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105); non-dominance concentrates among within-tier pairs (72.1\\% non-dominant, 31/43) versus cross-tier pairs (21.0\\%, 13/62), reflecting that capability-similar judges lack uniform ordering.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe theorem predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The independence-based intuition suggests $\\beta_1 \\leq 0$ under non-adaptive faults; the reversal pattern predicts positive $\\beta_1$ for judge-specific attacks and negative for shared-bias attacks. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges. We test these predictions across $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields a minimum detectable effect (MDE) of $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\\eta_{\\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\\eta_{\\max}$ disappears.}\n\\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ for $\\eta_{\\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \\to 0.437 \\to 0.401$), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift that does not depend on sample size or cluster count. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KHsRo6yqnRSiDiArwEwJau`</summary>

```text
1	% Mirror of abstract in main.tex — do not edit here.
2	
3	Multi-judge panels are widely deployed for robust Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation, yet adding judges can shift, not reduce, the dominant vulnerability pathway. Existing defenses conflate panel diversity with individual judge robustness, and it remains unclear which factor drives attack success rate (ASR) reduction. We decompose panel ASR into weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under ordinary least squares, but significance varies across inference methods (0 to 12 of 12), and the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal claims. The dominant predictor shifts with panel size: at $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates while $\eta_{\max}$ attenuates. Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Small panels benefit most from individual robustness screening; larger panels require attention to judge independence.
4	
```

  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely deployed for robust Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation, yet adding judges can shift, not reduce, the dominant vulnerability pathway. We decompose panel ASR into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is positive in all 12 conditions under ordinary least squares, but significance varies across inference methods (0 to 12 of 12), and the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal claims. The dominant predictor shifts with panel size: at $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ contribute; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates while $\\eta_{\\max}$ attenuates. Small panels benefit most from individual robustness screening; larger panels require attention to judge independence.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{=}7$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational.\n\\end{abstract}"
}

Capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\n\\textbf{Inference sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12, fractional logit 9/12), though coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes ($K{=}3$: 13, $K{=}5$: ${\\leq}11$, $K{=}7$: ${\\leq}9$ position clusters) \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; however, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains bootstrap-significant at $K{\\geq}5$ (7/12, 8/12), indicating that low cluster counts alone do not explain the attenuation pattern at $K{=}3$.\n\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation tasks (e.g., story writing, code generation) may present different attack surfaces and diversity--vulnerability relationships. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound the capability tier classification. Each attack type uses 1--2 template variants; template-specific effects could confound attack-type comparisons, and conclusions may not generalize beyond the tested wordings. All API models are accessed through OpenRouter, introducing a dependency on routing and snapshot consistency.\n\n\\textbf{Panel size range.} We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified. The 300-item sample per benchmark provides sufficient power for panel-level regression but may not capture the full distributional tail of judge vulnerabilities.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\nCode, the evaluation framework, and all panel-level results will be released upon acceptance. All evaluation items are sampled from publicly available benchmarks: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} and ARC-Challenge \\citep{clark2018arc}. The nine API-accessed judge models are served through OpenRouter; the six local judges are open-weight models (Qwen 2.5, Llama 3.1, Mistral Large) in AWQ-quantized form. Individual judge scores, panel compositions, and aggregated vulnerability metrics will be included in the release. All experiments use a fixed random seed (42), and all hyperparameters are reported in the methodology section to support reproducibility.\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is uniformly positively associated with vulnerability (both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity confirm positive direction in 12/12 conditions), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.264 \\to 0.101 \\to 0.084$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{=}7$). {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.256$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 across all $K$) (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.",
  "new_string": "We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, with the confidence interval crossing zero. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims."
}

    "newString": "We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the mechanism behind observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, a spectral measure of judge independence). Across all 12 conditions (two benchmarks $\\times$ six attacks), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s ordinary least-squares (OLS) coefficient is positive (12/12), directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, the significance of this association is method-sensitive (0 to 12 of 12 across inference approaches), and a previously uncharacterized transition emerges: at $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute (bootstrap significance: $\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two benchmarks, Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC), confirm this predictor shift. Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); adaptive attack reduces predictability to $R^2 = 0.256$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and generalized additive model (GAM) bootstrap), but the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (9/12 across all $K$) (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12, then 1/12) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial rather than complete (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings are descriptive; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) limits causal claims.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. 3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.93\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\eta_{\max}$ dominates. \textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \in (3,5)$. \textbf{(c)}~At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
```

{
