```json
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}",
    "newString": "\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \\textbf{(b)}~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.93\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The shifting bottleneck in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates. \\textbf{(b)}~A predictor shift emerges at $K^* \\in (3,5)$. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple independent judges---$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors: weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks---Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC)---reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity uniformly positively associated with vulnerability.} Both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} confirm a positive association with panel attack success in all 12 conditions (12/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.264$ [$0.158, 0.369$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.084$ [$-0.083, 0.251$] at $K{=}7$, with the confidence interval crossing zero. The diversity significance trajectory, however, depends on the metric: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases ($3 \\to 7 \\to 8$/12) while $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

          "+\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~At $K{=}3$, one compromised judge flips the majority---$\\eta_{\\max}$ is the strongest predictor. \\textbf{(b)}~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes as $K$ grows. \\textbf{(c)}~At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges---panel composition ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes more predictive than individual weakness.}",
```

```text
40	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}
41	\label{sec:cross_k}
42	
43	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 9/12).
44	
45	We repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
46	
47	\begin{figure}[t]
48	\centering
49	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
50	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
51	\label{fig:regime_transition}
52	\end{figure}
53	
54	\begin{figure}[t]
55	\centering
56	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dominance_heatmap.pdf}
57	\caption{Per-condition predictor dominance across panel sizes (wild cluster bootstrap, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$). At $K{=}3$, dominance is heterogeneous (co-dominant, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-only, $\eta_{\max}$-only, and non-significant conditions coexist); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates uniformly while $\eta_{\max}$ disappears.}
58	\label{fig:dominance_heatmap}
59	\end{figure}
60	
61	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$, with per-condition detail in Figure~\ref{fig:dominance_heatmap}. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with co-determination in two conditions. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Standardized coefficient magnitudes corroborate: $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ for $\eta_{\max}$ declines monotonically ($0.266 \to 0.101 \to 0.084$) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s trajectory peaks at $K{=}5$ ($0.349 \to 0.437 \to 0.401$), providing a power-independent characterization of the predictor shift that does not depend on sample size or cluster count. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.
62	
63	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
64	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
65	
66	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).
67	
68	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
69	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
```

Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.256$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 9/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",
